stay last, and~changes are~saved through the~normal ordering route.

When opening \ui{Edit gallery} in~the~right Admin drawer, ordinary settings
remain usable while photos load in~the~background. A~thin progress strip counts
processed thumbnails; \ui{Images} stays unavailable until the~complete list
and~all thumbnail requests have settled. Failed thumbnails receive placeholders
and~an~explicit \ui{Retry images} action; settings can~still be~saved. Only
the~small table thumbnails are~preloaded. Large previews remain on~demand,
and~the~File renamer prepares its~plan when its~tab is~opened. Closing
or~replacing the~drawer cancels obsolete work. Ordinary full-page navigation
remains the~complete fallback for~browsers without JavaScript.

The~gallery editor's \ui{Images} tab keeps common photo work in~one compact
list. Accessible thumbnail previews can~be~opened by~pointer, keyboard focus,
or~click; row selection supports a~Shift-click range. Each row provides direct
controls for~visibility, choosing the~gallery title picture, editing,
and~deleting that photo. These actions continue to~use the~existing
authenticated server workflows. The~\ui{Show file names} option changes only
the~editor display; administrators can~save an~optional browser-wide default,
while each gallery remembers its~choice for~the~current editor session.

The~title-picture star has three states when the~database supports multi-photo
covers: an~outline star selects that photo alone (gold); clicking the~gold star
again keeps it selected and~arms accumulation (blue); clicking another outline
star adds it to~the~selection, up~to~four photos. Clicking any selected blue
star keeps only that photo (gold). Each click saves immediately. The~right
drawer updates the~stars in~place, preserving its~open tab, scroll position,
other form drafts, and~loaded image rows; the~public gallery preview refreshes
separately. After a~lost or~failed response, the~editor first reads
the~authoritative cover state without changing the~panel. If the~state changed,
it reconciles the~stars before refreshing the~public gallery; otherwise read
the~current state before retrying, because persistence may have succeeded before
response construction failed.

Under \ui{Media management} > \ui{Title picture}, \ui{Automatic (2 photos)}
chooses up~to~two suitable direct photos and~fills missing positions from
accessible child galleries. The~original/default mode preserves legacy behavior
for~galleries that have not explicitly selected Automatic or~a~manual cover.
Public cards recheck current visibility and~gallery access; unpublished
or~inaccessible photos are~not~revealed.

When the~optional \ui{Picture Manager} feature is~enabled, a~normal signed-in
administrator can~select photographs and~direct physical subgalleries
in~the~public gallery grid. Checkmarks select individual cards; Shift-click
selects a~range; Ctrl-click or~Cmd-click toggles a~card; \ui{Select all},
\ui{Clear}, and~Ctrl/Cmd+A support larger visible-page selections. Smart Gallery
cards are~not~part of~this file-based selection. Dragging an~unselected photo
starts with that photo as~the~selection, while dragging an~existing photo
selection moves the~selected photographs together. A~visible child-gallery card
becomes a~drop target, and~dropping there performs the~same validated photo move
as~the~destination picker. Physical gallery cards themselves are~not~drag
sources.

From the~toolbar the~administrator can~delete a~mixed selection or~create a~real
physical gallery from it. Creation accepts a~searchable parent-gallery choice,
copies selected photographs into the~new root, and~copies selected physical
subgallery trees below it while leaving the~sources unchanged. The~server writes
current gallery sidecars, rejects recursive destinations and~existing target
folders, reserves each target root before copying, rebuilds copied gallery rows
from~the~filesystem, and~removes the~new partial result if~the~operation fails.
Mixed deletion removes selected photographs through normal image cleanup
and~applies the~configured recoverable Trash policy to~selected gallery trees
when available.

Move and~copy-to-existing-gallery remain photo-only actions. If~a~physical
gallery is~selected, clear it~before using those controls. Photo selections
can~also be~shared or~downloaded. \ui{Share} fetches the~authorized
browser-displayable image versions as~browser \codeword{File} objects and~opens
the~native Web Share sheet when the~browser/device supports multi-file sharing.
The~browser chooses the~available receiving apps, so~PHP Gallery cannot force
Instagram Story, Reel, or~another specific target. If~native sharing
is~unavailable, cannot accept the~files, or~fails after preparation, the~manager
starts the~authenticated selected-photo ZIP download as~a~fallback. The~same ZIP
endpoint can~therefore serve both an~explicit selected-photo download and~share
fallback. Ordinary visitors never receive these controls, and~the~server
revalidates the~logged-in account, CSRF token, source ownership, direct-child
gallery ownership, and~destination gallery for~every mutation or~ZIP request.

\subsection{Uploads and~discovery}
\index{discovery}

Filesystem discovery is~useful when photographs have already been copied
to~the~server through FTP, a~hosting file manager, or~a~restored backup.
The~discovery screen shows what the~application found and~lets the~administrator
bring those folders and~files into the~catalog. Discovery runs
as~a~session-backed browser job in~small AJAX batches rather than scanning
the~entire tree during the~initial Admin page request. The~current default
examines up~to~80 directories per~batch, accepts a~bounded batch size up~to~300,
and~expires abandoned discovery job state after two hours. This keeps a~large
folder tree responsive on~slower hosting while still showing progress
and~a~final actionable result list.

The~scanner walks below the~configured gallery root and~does not~follow
directory symlinks. Hidden directories and~known generated/cache directory names
such as~\filepath{cache}, \filepath{thumbs}, \filepath{thumbnail},
\filepath{thumbnails}, \filepath{preview}, and~\filepath{previews} are~ignored.
A~new candidate branch must contain at~least one supported image somewhere below
it. Empty structural parent folders can~still become galleries when they
are~needed to~preserve the~hierarchy above image-bearing descendants. A~folder
containing only a~\filepath{gallery.json} sidecar and~no~supported image
anywhere in~its branch is~reported as~metadata-only rather than imported
as~a~gallery by~the~current discovery workflow. Already indexed gallery folders
are~suppressed from the~candidate list, and~an~import candidate whose display
title would duplicate an~existing sibling title is~not~silently created.

For~each unmanaged result, the~administrator can~import the~folder tree
in~place, move supported photographs from selected unmanaged folders into
an~existing gallery, or~delete selected unmanaged folders from~disk. Import
expands the~selection in~parent-to-child order and~can~include missing
image-bearing ancestors and~descendants. The~move action avoids overwriting
an~existing destination filename by~choosing a~deterministic unique name, scans
the~destination gallery after successful moves, and~removes source directories
only when they become empty. The~discovery-specific disk-delete action
is~deliberately restricted to~safe non-root unmanaged paths below the~gallery
root and~refuses folders that are~already represented by~database galleries. Use
the~normal gallery deletion workflow for~an~imported CMS gallery.

\subsubsection{The~gallery.json metadata sidecar}
\index{gallery.json}
\index{sidecar metadata}

PHP Gallery keeps its active catalog and~relationships in~the~database, while
an~optional \filepath{gallery.json} file beside a~gallery folder preserves
useful editable metadata with the~filesystem. Normal gallery saves write
the~sidecar back to~the~gallery folder. Current sidecar output includes
the~title, description, normalized gallery tags, visibility, order, voting
and~filename-display state, maintained content language/translations when
available, manual gallery dates, selected card/count/lightbox/grid overrides,
responsive-thumbnail bounds, access/listing policy, cover references,
and~configured gallery branding assets where those schemas are~available.
Sidecar writing follows the~same schema checks as~the~corresponding gallery
features.

When Admin tag sidecar repair runs, it refuses targets outside the~configured
gallery root or targets it cannot verify; links resolving inside remain usable.
A~refusal or failed sidecar write does not record its completion checkpoint.
The~writer refuses multiple hard links or unusable inode identity and checks
the~opened target and prior content before truncation; a~later write failure
or~interruption can~leave partial content. The~whole JSON document is~decoded
and~re-encoded, so~formatting and~number representation may change. These
checks do~not~exclude unrelated filesystem changes between observations.

During discovery and~missing-parent repair, PHP Gallery can~read supported
values from~the~sidecar instead of~deriving everything from the~folder name.
Imported rows conservatively default to~unpublished when no~visibility
is~specified. The~current import path restores the~sidecar fields explicitly
supported by~the~importer, including title/description/localization, visibility,
manual dates, voting/filename presentation, selected
card/count/lightbox/grid/thumbnail overrides, access/listing policy, order,
and~branding paths. The~sidecar should therefore be~treated as~portable
gallery-folder metadata, not~as~a~complete database backup: relational state,
credentials, logs, votes, game state, Smart Gallery definitions, and~other
database-owned records still require the~normal backup procedure.

\securitynote{The~\ui{Delete from disk} action on~the~discovery screen is~a~real
recursive filesystem deletion for~unmanaged candidate folders. Review
the~selected relative paths before confirming it. The~guard against known
imported gallery paths is~an~additional safety boundary, not~a~replacement
for~a~backup.}

Browser upload is~the~friendliest choice for~routine work. It~can~upload into
an~existing gallery or~create a~child gallery and~upload optional photographs
in~the~same side-panel workflow. Select the~destination, choose files,
and~follow the~progress display. The~application checks the~destination and~file
information before accepting each result.

With JavaScript enabled, focus the~upload area and~paste an~image or~screenshot
using Ctrl+V on~Windows/Linux or~Cmd+V on~macOS. The~focused or~most recently
used visible uploader receives the~images; an~open side panel owns its upload
target. Paste appends all supported image items exposed by~the~browser
to~the~same file selection used by~the~chooser. JPEG, PNG, GIF and~WebP follow
the~picker policy; HEIC, HEIF and~DNG additionally require an~explicit
server-capability hint. Browser-provided filenames are~retained; unnamed image
data receives a~distinct timestamp/sequence name with the~matching extension.
Submit the~ordinary form to~upload through the~selected browser/server pipeline,
with its usual validation, size limits, metadata, thumbnails, progress
and~errors. Text fields keep ordinary text paste; unsupported content and~paste
during an~active upload are~ignored. If~the~browser cannot expose or~add
clipboard images, use the~chooser or~file drop. Side-panel uploads retain
the~open panel and~current URL.

In~the~right-hand upload drawer, repeated paste, file-picker choices and~drop
append to~one local preview queue. Inspect its count and~total size; use Remove,
Clear selection or~the~arrow buttons to~adjust the~FileList and~upload order.
Then press the~ordinary Upload button once. Both existing-gallery
and~create-with-upload drawers use this queue; the~dedicated full-page picker
keeps its established behavior. The~queue does not~create a~server draft
and~has~no~Commit publication action.

Preview work is~lazy and~serialized: one decoder, at~most twelve miniature
resources, and~PNG miniatures no~larger than 256 pixels per~edge. Before
decoding, only the~first 256 KiB of~headers are~inspected; a~source must fit
within 24 MiB, 8192 pixels per~edge and~16 * 1024 * 1024 pixels overall.
Unsupported, animated, unreadable or~larger candidates show an~icon or~warning
while the~original remains selected. These preview limits do~not~replace server
upload limits. Selection never strips EXIF or~replaces the~original with
a~miniature.

Repeated filenames receive a~warning but~remain separate selected occurrences;
removing a~duplicate updates that warning. Empty files, unavailable previews
and~format/size warnings do~not~erase healthy files. A~disabled file input also
blocks clipboard and~drop additions. The~displayed total describes the~local
selection: classic uploads send one image per~request and~browser preparation
follows its configured batch limits.

Closing or~switching the~drawer offers an~explicit choice for~unsent files;
Escape cancels that warning and~restores focus. Native Reset clears only
an~unsubmitted selection. After an~uncertain or~partially failed upload,
removal, replacement and~Reset are~blocked to~preserve the~exact retry inputs
and~operation keys. Retry from the~same form; only canonical acknowledgment
clears the~queue. Navigation releases preview resources. A~browser history-cache
return may~restore page state, but~an~ordinary reload does not~retain unsent
files.

Compressed file size alone does~not~prove that an~image is~safe to~decode.
Before GD or~optional Imagick allocates a~full raster, PHP Gallery checks
the~format, dimensions, pixel arithmetic, configured/runtime memory budget,
and~pipeline limits. A~file that cannot~be~admitted safely is~refused before its
original or~generated derivatives are~committed to~the~target gallery. This
policy applies to~classic and~browser-prepared ingestion; it~does not~claim that
every external RAW decoder has~the~same in-process memory model.

The~upload settings distinguish the~ordinary server path from~browser-assisted
preparation. \ui{Allow all server-supported formats} leaves the~picker aligned
with~the~server's detected capabilities. \ui{Prefer phone-rendered JPG/PNG/WebP,
no RAW/DNG} asks mobile browsers for~a~rendered image representation when
possible, which is~useful when an~iPhone ProRAW/DNG workflow produces unsuitable
color. The~browser hint is~not~a~guaranteed transcoder, so~the~server still
validates the~actual uploaded media.

Browser-assisted preparation is~enabled independently and~is~checked by~default
in~the~upload workflow. The~browser can~prepare optimized thumbnails and~bounded
ZIP batches before sending them. If~browser preparation fails before any
server-side write starts, ordinary image selections can~fall back automatically
to~the~classic server upload path. Administrators can~bound the~default
and~maximum browser worker counts, images per~batch, ZIP-to-PHP-upload-limit
ratio, maximum ZIP batch bytes, and~source chunk size used by~browser-side
thumbnail rebuild. The~normalizer also enforces a~hard worker cap
so~an~accidental setting cannot create unbounded parallel requests on~shared
hosting.

Server-side acceptance of~browser-prepared ZIP batches also verifies the~CRC-32
of~every stored file and~requires a~complete central directory and~end record
matching entry names, offsets, sizes, and~counts. A~damaged or~inconsistent
prepared batch is~rejected before gallery files are~written; rebuild and~retry
the~batch in~the~browser.

Current browser-pipeline defaults are~8 workers, an~80\% target of~the~detected
PHP upload limit for~each store-only ZIP batch, at~most 8 images per~ZIP batch,
and~a~24~MiB absolute ZIP-batch cap. The~validated ranges are~1--32 workers,
10--95\% of~the~PHP upload limit, 1--64 images per~batch, and~1--128~MiB
for~the~absolute ZIP cap. Browser thumbnail rebuilding uses a~512~MiB source-ZIP
chunk by~default, clamped to~16~MiB--3~GiB, and~the~source builder normally caps
a~chunk at~96 items with an~internal hard limit of~512. These are safety
ceilings and~defaults, not~a~recommendation to~raise a~slow shared host
to~the~maximum.

When browser-assisted upload is~enabled, the~chooser also accepts ZIP archives
such as~an~iCloud Photos export. The~browser inspects the~archive locally, adds
supported JPEG, PNG, WebP, and~GIF images to~the~ordinary preparation queue,
and~skips folders, hidden metadata, and~unsupported entries. The~selected ZIP
itself is~never uploaded to~PHP. Stored and~Deflate archives are~supported;
encrypted, multi-disk, ZIP64, damaged, suspiciously compressed, or~excessively
large archives are~rejected before any server-side upload begins. ZIP import
has~no~classic-PHP fallback, so~keep browser-assisted preparation enabled when
selecting an~archive.

The~global automatic-upload rename option can~run the~same post-scan naming
policy used by~the~Media Renamer after browser, API, or~WebDAV ingestion. Leave
it~off when external filenames are~part of~your archival convention; enable
it~when all arrival paths should converge on~the~gallery's normalized naming
scheme.

\subsection{Generated metadata and~automation}
\index{AI metadata}
\index{OpenAI}
\index{upload automation}
\index{WebDAV}

The~optional local or~worker-based image-analysis queue can~record visual
metadata separately from the~human title, description, tags, and~EXIF fields.
Administrators can~review that metadata in~the~photo editor and~use
it~as~searchable evidence without silently replacing editorial text. Configured
OpenAI assistance can~generate a~photo or~gallery description, clean up~existing
text, summarize context, describe visible content from safe generated
thumbnails, or~suggest a~translation. These actions insert reviewable drafts;
they do~not~publish or~save text automatically unless the~administrator
completes the~ordinary save operation.

For~repeated transfers, create a~scoped upload-automation token for~the~intended
gallery and~send supported files to~the~upload endpoint. Tokens can~be~revoked
independently and~must be~treated like passwords. The~raw API key is~shown only
when it~is~created; only its hash is~stored afterward, so~copy the~new key
to~the~intended client at~creation time. The~global \ui{API manager} lists
automation credentials across galleries and~provides a~central revocation
workflow without exposing the~original secrets. Gallery-level controls remain
available for~the~same scoped credentials.

The~public discovery query at~the~installation-relative address
\texttt{index.php?page=app\_linking\_discovery} reports version~1 and~checks
the~canonical HTTPS issuer, independent secret, core schema and~optional
capability readiness. If~core linking is~not~ready it~reports unavailable;
unknown optional storage hides only that operation. It~does not~require an
Admin session or~reveal credentials or~gallery lists. The~existing
gallery-scoped API key remains independent; discovery does not~authorize
uploads.

The~Windows companion uses the~same gallery-scoped upload contract. It~has~two
normal upload modes. \ui{Watch folder} ignores files that were already present
when watching begins, waits for~new files to~become stable, remembers confirmed
uploads locally, and~retries transient failures with backoff. It~can~optionally
attach the~current Microsoft Flight Simulator camera or~aircraft
latitude/longitude/altitude through SimConnect immediately before each watched
upload, and~can~delete a~watched source only after the~gallery confirms
a~successful upload. \ui{Manual upload} accepts a~file selection independently
of~the~watch folder and~can~either generate responsive thumbnails on~the~PC with
worker processes or~ask the~gallery server to~generate them. Thumbnail work
and~network uploads are~pipelined rather than~staging an~entire large selection
first. Tray support lets the~watcher or~manual job continue while the~main
window is~hidden.

The~same Windows application can~optionally run a~low-priority AI metadata
worker. It~claims one authenticated analysis job, downloads only the~claimed
asset, produces internal searchable metadata with the~selected local
backend/model generation, and~returns the~result to~the~existing
upload-automation endpoint. This worker is~separate from~human captions.
Stopping waits until any in-flight heartbeat request actually ends; blocked
HTTP or~DNS can~extend this wait beyond the~socket timeout.
A~gallery-level \ui{Force AI metadata regeneration} action clears the~stored
generated rows/old queue state for~that gallery branch and~queues fresh work;
the~heavy analysis still runs on~the~Windows worker.

Mobile WebDAV credentials are~gallery-scoped and~use HTTP Basic authentication
plus an~unguessable path token. Creating a~connection shows its generated
password once; the~server keeps only a~password hash afterward. The~Admin page
lists the~connection label, target gallery, server URL, and~last-use time
and~can~revoke the~connection. The~WebDAV endpoint supports the~minimal
\codeword{OPTIONS}, \codeword{PROPFIND}, \codeword{MKCOL}, and~\codeword{PUT}
behavior needed by~photo-transfer clients such as~PhotoSync. Current WebDAV
ingestion accepts JPG, PNG, GIF, and~WebP. Configure the~mobile client
to~convert HEIC to~JPEG before transfer where available. Uploaded media then
enters the~same scan, optional auto-rename, database, and~thumbnail pipeline
as~other uploads.

\section{Thumbnail generation and~public rendering}
\label{sec:thumbnails}
\index{thumbnails}
\index{WebP}
\index{JPEG}
\index{image previews}
\index{page speed!thumbnails}

\subsection{What a~thumbnail is}
\index{thumbnail!definition}

A~thumbnail is~a~smaller display copy of~a~photograph. The~original photograph
may~be~several thousand pixels wide and~occupy many megabytes. A~gallery card
on~a~phone may~need only a~few hundred pixels. Sending the~full original
for~every small card would make visitors wait for~detail they cannot see at~that
size.\footnote{Thumbnail generation keeps the~source photograph as~the~archival
and~full-viewing asset. The~generated copy is~a~replaceable browsing aid.}

Imagine a~gallery containing 60 photographs, each with a 12-megabyte original
file. Loading every original for~the~first grid could require hundreds
of~megabytes. Small thumbnails can~reduce that first viewing task dramatically.
The~original remains available for~authorized full viewing or~download, while
the~grid receives efficient previews.\footnote{Actual savings depend on~image
content, quality, format, dimensions, and~the~browser's selected candidate.
The~example illustrates scale rather than promising one fixed compression
ratio.}

Thumbnails serve several everyday purposes:

\begin{itemize}
  \item gallery grids appear sooner;
  \item scrolling uses less memory and~network capacity;
  \item mobile visitors receive an~image closer to~the~size of~their screen;
  \item gallery covers and~search results remain compact;
  \item the~large viewer can~begin with a~suitable preview while a~larger source
  is~prepared;
  \item repeated visits can~reuse files already stored in~the~browser
  cache.\footnote{A~browser cache stores recently used web resources
  on~the~visitor's device according to~server and~browser policy. Reuse can~make
  a~later visit feel much quicker.}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Why several thumbnail sizes exist}
\index{thumbnail!sizes}
\index{device pixel ratio}

One small size cannot suit every screen. A~narrow phone card may~look clear with
a 300-pixel thumbnail. A~wide desktop card may~need 600 or 800 pixels.
A~high-density screen uses more image pixels to~draw the~same physical area
and~may~benefit from a~larger candidate.

PHP Gallery can~prepare common sizes such as 300, 600, 800, 960, 1280, and 1600
pixels, depending on~configuration and~source-image limits. The~number describes
the~maximum long side of~the~generated copy. Portrait and~landscape photographs
keep their original proportions.\footnote{For~a~landscape photograph, the~long
side is~usually the~width. For~a~portrait photograph, it~is~usually the~height.
The~shorter side is~calculated from the~original aspect ratio.}

The~smaller candidates serve grids and~covers. Larger candidates serve wide
cards, high-density displays, search presentations, map previews, and~the~large
viewer. The~browser can~select from the~available group instead of~receiving one
fixed size for~every situation.

\subsection{Why JPEG and~WebP are~available}
\index{thumbnail!formats}
\index{JPEG!thumbnail use}
\index{WebP!thumbnail use}

JPEG is~widely compatible and~familiar for~photographs. WebP often provides
similar visual quality with fewer transferred bytes. PHP Gallery can~generate
both so~a~compatible browser can~use WebP while JPEG remains a~dependable
alternative.\footnote{Compression efficiency varies with the~photograph
and~quality settings. Fine texture, noise, gradients, and~sharp graphic details
can~produce different results.}

The~visitor does not~choose a~format manually. The~page tells the~browser which
versions exist, and~the~browser chooses a~format it~understands. The~original
photograph keeps its own source format and~remains separate from these browsing
copies.

\subsection{When thumbnails are~created}
\index{thumbnail!generation}
\index{thumbnail!maintenance}

Thumbnails can~be~prepared when photographs are~imported or~uploaded, rebuilt
through Admin maintenance, or~generated through a~guarded repair process when
the~public page discovers that an~expected copy is~missing. Preparing them near
upload time gives later visitors the~smoothest experience because the~required
files already exist before the~first visit.\footnote{Large imports can~require
meaningful processing time and~storage. Running preparation as~an~intentional
Admin task gives the~owner a~clearer opportunity to~monitor completion.}

A~gallery owner should review thumbnail maintenance after:

\begin{itemize}
  \item importing a~large existing folder tree;
  \item changing enabled thumbnail sizes or~quality;
  \item restoring a~backup that contains originals and~database data
  but~an~incomplete thumbnail cache;
  \item moving an~installation between servers with different image-format
  support;
  \item seeing repeated original-file fallbacks or~missing previews in~System
  Health.
\end{itemize}

Generated thumbnails are~reproducible cache data and~can~be~rebuilt from
the~source photographs. The~sources themselves require permanent backup
protection.

\subsection{DNG and~RAW display masters}
\index{DNG}
\index{RAW photographs}

DNG photographs can~remain the~archival source while PHP Gallery creates
a~browser-display master for~thumbnail generation and~public viewing.
The~display master is~generated derivative data, not~a~replacement
for~the~original DNG. If~the~required local conversion tool is~unavailable
or~conversion fails, the~Admin upload and~thumbnail reports identify the~failed
DNG derivative. Thumbnail maintenance can~rebuild the~master later; deleting
generated derivatives does~not~delete the~source DNG.

\ui{Runtime diagnostics} exposes the~active DNG conversion policy together with
the~server's actual GD, Imagick/ImageMagick, and~format capabilities. The~source
policy can~use automatic fallback with full RAW decoding first and~the~embedded
camera preview second, explicitly prefer full RAW decode, or~prefer the~embedded
preview. Color handling can~force browser-safe sRGB, preserve the~decoded
appearance as~closely as~possible, or~use camera white balance when available.
These choices affect generated browser derivatives only. They never rewrite
the~archival DNG source, and~their availability still depends on~the~image
delegates and~memory limits provided by~the~host.

\subsection{What visitors receive}
\index{thumbnail!browser selection}

The~gallery sends the~browser a~list of~thumbnail copies that really exist
for~a~card. The~browser considers the~space available on~the~page, the~sharpness
needs of~the~screen, the~supported format, and~its own loading decisions.
It~then requests an~appropriate file.

For~example, suppose a~photograph has 300, 600, 800, and 960-pixel copies:

\begin{itemize}
  \item a~small phone card may~receive the 300-pixel copy;
  \item a~medium card may~receive the 600-pixel copy;
  \item a~large or~high-density card may~receive the 800 or 960-pixel copy;
  \item the~large viewer may~request a~still larger preview prepared for~that
  purpose.
\end{itemize}

The~exact choice can~differ between browsers and~screens. This flexibility
is~useful because the~server does not~need to~guess one universal size.

\subsection{Responsive browser selection}
\index{responsive renderer}
\index{srcset}
\index{thumbnail!responsive mode}

\ui{Responsive browser selection} is~the~supported legacy mode under \ui{Admin >
Theme > Layout}. The~page exposes the~available thumbnail candidates immediately
and~the~browser chooses the~one that best matches the~rendered card and~display
density. JavaScript is~not~required for~this selection.\footnote{The~underlying
web standard calls the~candidate list a~\emph{srcset}. Gallery owners can~rely
on~the~visual behavior without learning that markup term.}

Responsive selection usually transfers one chosen thumbnail for~each loaded card
and~is~the~normal choice when predictable browser-native behavior matters most.

\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.30\textwidth} X}
\toprule
\textbf{Situation} & \textbf{Likely result}\\
\midrule
Narrow mobile card & A~smaller thumbnail candidate is~usually sufficient.\\
Wide desktop card & The~browser may~choose a~medium or~larger candidate.\\
High-density display & A~higher-resolution candidate may~be~selected
for~the~same CSS size.\\
JavaScript unavailable & Responsive candidate selection still works.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}

The 300-pixel copy acts as~a~fallback when available. Its presence does
not~force a~modern browser to~download it~first because the~larger choices
are~presented at~the~same time.

\subsection{Progressive thumbnail sharpening}
\index{progressive renderer}
\index{progressive sharpening}
\index{thumbnail!progressive mode}

\ui{Progressive thumbnail sharpening} is~the~default and~safe fallback under
\ui{Admin > Theme > Layout}. The~gallery first presents a~small thumbnail,
usually 300 pixels, and~later replaces relevant cards with larger copies.
On~slower connections this can~make the~collection recognizable earlier,
at~the~cost of~potentially transferring both the~small and~replacement
thumbnail.

\subsubsection{What happens on~the~screen}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item The~page reserves the~final card geometry.
  \item Small thumbnails begin filling visible cards.
  \item Relevant cards are~queued for~a~larger candidate.
  \item The~larger copy loads while the~small one remains visible.
  \item The~card is~replaced after the~larger copy is~ready.
\end{enumerate}

Larger upgrades are~queued rather than started for~every card at~once,
and~visible cards receive priority over distant content. The~current progressive
option applies to~photo cards inside an~open gallery. Gallery covers, collage
cells, home-page gallery cards, and~Admin images continue using responsive
browser selection. The~Admin interface labels progressive sharpening as~Default
and~responsive selection as~Legacy.

\subsubsection{The~transfer tradeoff}

Progressive mode can~transfer two files for~one photograph: the~small first copy
and~the~sharper replacement. Responsive mode often transfers one
browser-selected copy. Progressive mode therefore favors earlier visual
population, while responsive mode usually reduces total transfer for~the~same
card.

\subsection{Which mode should an~owner choose?}
\index{thumbnail!choosing a renderer}

Choose \ui{Responsive browser selection} when efficient browser-native selection
and~typically one thumbnail transfer per~loaded card suit the~collection.

Keep \ui{Progressive thumbnail sharpening} for~a~small first image followed
by~bounded upgrades on~relevant cards. Test both supported renderers with
representative galleries and~devices when choosing the~best presentation
for~your visitors.

Useful questions include:

\begin{itemize}
  \item Do~visitors see the~first photographs sooner?
  \item Does the~small version look acceptable during sharpening?
  \item How much extra data is~transferred on~a~typical visit?
  \item Do~phones and~high-density screens sharpen at~a~comfortable pace?
  \item Does the~chosen mode suit the~audience's likely connection quality?
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Loading priorities and~stable layout}
\index{lazy loading}
\index{layout stability}
\index{thumbnail!lazy loading}

The~first visible photographs receive earlier loading attention because they
shape the~visitor's first impression. Photographs farther down the~page use lazy
loading, which means the~browser can~wait until they approach the~visible area.
This avoids spending connection capacity on~the~bottom of~a~long gallery while
the~visitor is~still looking at~the~top.\footnote{Lazy loading timing belongs
partly to~the~browser. Browsers consider scrolling, viewport distance,
connection state, and~their own implementation policy.}

The~gallery knows the~width and~height of~indexed photographs. It~uses those
proportions to~reserve stable card shapes before image loading finishes. Text,
buttons, and~neighboring cards therefore keep their positions as~thumbnails
appear.

Visitors who prefer reduced motion receive a~calm presentation without
a~required continuous loading animation. Photograph links remain usable
throughout loading and~sharpening.

\subsection{Thumbnail quality and~storage}
\index{thumbnail!quality}
\index{thumbnail!storage}

Higher quality preserves more fine detail and~can~create larger files. Lower
quality saves space and~transfer while introducing more visible compression.
The~best setting depends on~subject matter, screen use, hosting capacity,
and~audience. Detailed foliage, hair, text, and~fine architecture can~reveal
compression more readily than broad soft backgrounds.

Every additional enabled size and~format uses storage because it~creates another
copy for~each photograph. The~benefit is~more precise delivery to~different
screens. System Health and~storage statistics help an~owner understand
the~resulting cache. A~large source archive may~produce a~substantial thumbnail
collection, yet~those files remain reproducible from the~originals.

\subsection{Thumbnail compatibility and~maintenance modes}
\index{thumbnail compatibility}
\index{thumbnail maintenance}
\index{browser-side thumbnail rebuild}

The~maintenance panel has~two compatibility policies. \ui{Modern} generates WebP
thumbnails wherever the~server can~write WebP. \ui{Legacy} generates JPEG
fallback thumbnails as~well as~WebP. After switching an~installation permanently
to~Modern, \ui{Remove legacy JPG thumbnails} deletes only generated JPEG
thumbnail files; originals, WebP derivatives, and~DNG display masters
are~preserved.

\textbf{Gallery consistency.} Open \ui{Maintenance~Center} >
\ui{Gallery~consistency}. Its~tabs group Installation, System tools
and~Gallery consistency. The~former Overview maintenance tools, including
media renaming and~thumbnail controls, now live in~System tools. Saved
maintenance links redirect here; the~main vertical menu gains no~item.
\ui{Analyze~gallery} first creates a~read-only report of~physical folders,
primary names, parent relationships, sidecar titles, original files, expected
thumbnails and~their metadata. It~also finds unindexed photographs, unowned
derivatives and~pending image moves. Missing originals and~unavailable
evidence remain explicit findings.

The Overview shortcut retains System Health warnings beside the Maintenance
Center title.

The~initial scope is~\ui{Names and~paths (DB~only)}: it~compares gallery
metadata without opening photographs or~physical folders. \ui{Files
and~thumbnails} checks real files, headers, times and~metadata in~batches.
\ui{Deep content verification (slow)} additionally reads checksums and~decodes
pixels; large collections require substantially more work. Each report names
its~actual scope. Expanded rows show \ui{Now} and~\ui{After repair} values
for~proposed title, path, parent or~sidecar changes, before any repair
is~chosen.

Findings are~grouped by~reason with exact counts and~a~short action or~manual
status. Photo/file examples combine a~known original's variants under its
catalog filename; technical cache names and~path hashes stay in~nested details.
A~question mark explains the~current state, why~it~matters and~the~repair result.
Hover or~focus opens help, activation pins it, and~Escape or~outside click
closes it. Native disclosures remain available. Unassigned previews remain
manual; unused size/format records may~be~historical, not~damaged images.
Only bounded displayed photo IDs are~looked up~in~the~catalog, without reading
photo files; names-only reports perform no~photo lookup. The~export stays complete.

Only affected galleries appear in~compact expandable rows. Select individual
galleries or~use \ui{Repair~all~available}; manual findings remain separate.
Catalog progress counts records completed in~batches, disk discovery shows
its~actual entry count without inventing a~total, and~repairs confirm one task
per~batch. The~JSON export includes all findings beyond the~paged list
and~detail samples. The~browser needs JavaScript and~keeps its~checkpoint
in~the~Admin session.

Repairs revalidate catalog and~source ownership, preserve originals and~valid
thumbnails, and~stop visibly on~failure. Lost responses require a~status
check, never automatic write replay. Confirmed findings disappear after each
successful batch; empty gallery rows vanish. Select another subset immediately
without a~new analysis. Manual and~unselected findings remain; exported receipts
retain history. Older completed jobs adopt this list on~opening. Catalog
proposals refresh from DB without inspecting photos again. Delayed earlier
batch requests are~refused; external catalog changes still require a~new report.
Small
RGB comparisons flag suspicious image content for~manual review; similarity
does not~prove image identity. Unsupported decoding, ambiguous files
and~missing originals are~not~automatically repaired. Hidden/internal storage
and~non-image attachments retain their separate maintenance tools.

Disk names also govern import. A~sidecar title survives only when
its~sanitized form agrees with the~physical leaf. Otherwise the~disk leaf
supplies the~title. New imported folders are~canonicalized deepest first
through the~guarded subtree mover. Conflicting destinations refuse import;
unrelated existing galleries are~not~silently renamed. Explicit consistency
repair handles historical discrepancies.

A~browser failure remains visible and~never starts server generation silently.
This choice applies to~both Create all and~Create missing; the~global warning
opens these controls first. Consistency repair always uses browser workers.
Keep the~tab open. Only requested variants are~generated and~published;
valid unrelated files retain their bytes and~timestamps. The~server validates
source identity and~stored output before advancing the~task count. Lost
replies first reconcile stored output. Unsupported RAW/HEIC sources and~browser
errors remain visible without PHP generation fallback. Results retain a~labeled
list of~remaining findings and~show confirmed variant counts.
Browser processing progress sits with the~main progress above the~finding list.

A~full \ui{Check missing thumbnails} pass inspects imported photographs
and~records galleries with missing, stale, or~wrong-ratio generated variants
without creating anything. The~stored result enables \ui{Create missing
thumbnails}, which targets only the~reported images. \ui{Create all
thumbnails} in~Maintenance covers all galleries; the~same action
in~a~gallery's Images editor covers that gallery and, when \ui{Include
subgalleries} is~selected, its descendants. These Admin operations remain
the~primary deliberate maintenance workflow. Public browsing does~not~start
an~unbounded repair sweep, but~the~current renderer has~a~small guarded
background warmup for~specific cards that had~to~fall back to~authorized
original media because an~expected derivative was~missing.

\ui{Refresh thumbnail database} reconciles database metadata with existing
generated files and~removes wrong-ratio generated files instead of~publishing
them. \ui{Delete all thumbnails} is~a~separately confirmed destructive cache
operation; source photographs remain untouched and~the~derivatives
can~be~rebuilt later.

\ui{Browser-side thumbnail rebuild} is~selected by~default for~these
regeneration actions when browser processing is~available. Clear its checkbox
to~use server generation. The~server sends original files to~the~authenticated
browser in~bounded source ZIP chunks, the~browser generates thumbnail
variants, and~prepared thumbnail ZIP batches are~uploaded back for~server
validation and~installation. The~source-chunk and~batch limits are~configured
with the~browser-upload settings so~the~workflow does~not~depend on~one
oversized PHP request.

\subsubsection{Guarded public thumbnail warmup}
\index{thumbnail warmup}
\index{thumbnail!self-healing}

When the~server has~rendered a~publicly authorized photograph through
an~original-media fallback, it~can~mark that exact photograph and~the~missing
supported sizes as~a~background warmup candidate. The~browser waits for~idle
time, stops when the~page is~hidden, sends at~most two candidates per~request,
makes at~most four requests from one page load, and~spaces requests by~about 1.8
seconds. The~server independently caps the~normalized submitted list at~24 image
IDs, but~the~actual generation budget is~smaller: an~anonymous request processes
at~most two source images within roughly four seconds, while an~authenticated
administrator request processes at~most four within roughly eight seconds.

A~candidate carries an~HMAC token tied to~the~image ID, gallery ID, filename,
relative path, and~gallery folder that the~server originally rendered. Before
generating anything, the~endpoint reloads the~image/gallery, verifies that
token, repeats current visitor access checks, normalizes the~requested sizes
to~the~configured thumbnail set, consults thumbnail metadata when available,
and~skips variants that are~already renderable. A~per-image 60-second cooldown
prevents repeated expensive retries. A~single global non-waiting worker lock
means concurrent public traffic cannot stack several image-decoding jobs; a~busy
request simply reports that another warmup worker is~active. The~warmup
is~enabled by~default through the~internal
\setting{thumbnail_background_warmup_enabled} application setting and~writes
bounded diagnostic events to~the~Admin log when logging storage is~available.

\securitynote{Background warmup does not~turn a~private or~otherwise
inaccessible photograph into~a~public repair target. The~signed candidate proves
that the~server rendered that image as~a~fallback candidate, and~the~endpoint
still repeats the~current access policy before opening the~source or~writing
derivatives.}

\subsection{If~a~thumbnail is~missing}
\index{thumbnail!missing}
\index{thumbnail!rebuild}

A~missing thumbnail may~appear as~a~slower original-file fallback, an~empty
preview, or~a~maintenance warning, depending on~the~image and~available files.
Open the~thumbnail maintenance tools, inspect the~affected gallery or~sizes,
and~rebuild the~missing variants. Review write permissions and~image-format
support when rebuilding repeatedly fails.

After repair, open the~gallery with an~empty browser cache and~confirm that
covers, cards, and~the~large viewer receive the~expected previews.

\section{Lightbox, maps, EXIF, and~voting}
\label{sec:lightbox}
\index{lightbox}
\index{EXIF}
\index{GPS}
\index{voting}

\subsection{Compact browsing and~keyboard focus}

The~photo viewer fits narrow screens, with the~counter separated from~its
controls. Opening a~photo or~map moves keyboard focus into~that layer;
\ui{Tab} and~\ui{Shift+Tab} stay inside it. \ui{Escape} closes the~map before
the~underlying photo, and~closing returns focus to~the~opening control.
At~100\% a~single finger swipes between photos; above that scale one finger
pans and~two fingers pinch. Closing a~direct photo restores the~album context
for~language switching and~login return links. Map actions without available
location data are~hidden. Interactive markers identify route starts,
destinations, waypoints or~photo locations.

Tag results use readable single-column cards on~narrow screens. Search
suggestions show plain description text while retaining useful link labels.
Authored photo captions/titles take precedence over a~localized numbered
fallback. Public controls and~media/map panels follow the~selected Theme's
foreground and~panel colors. The~picture game retains an~album breadcrumb
link and~compact photo comparisons. Floating navigation uses small icons;
widgets retain their saved placement and~return to~the~page flow when
previews or~controls leave no~safe floating space.

\subsection{Asynchronous lightbox metadata}

The~lightbox is~the~large photograph viewer that opens above the~gallery page.
It~lets visitors concentrate on~one photograph while retaining forward,
backward, close, full-screen, slideshow, map, and~other available controls.

The~gallery prepares the~viewer when it~becomes useful and~obtains information
for~nearby photographs in~manageable groups. A~visitor can~therefore move
through a~large gallery without requiring the~first page to~carry every title,
preview, map point, and~vote at~once.\footnote{The~current viewer normally
requests information in~nearby groups of~photographs. Access checks are~repeated
for~each request so~private gallery rules continue to~apply.}

The~Left and~Right arrow keys move one photograph backward or~forward.
\codeword{Shift+Left} and~\codeword{Shift+Right} move ten positions for~faster
travel through a~large result set. The~jump follows the~same stable ordered
sequence as~ordinary navigation, wraps at~the~ends, and~also works when
the~active result set is~a~Smart Gallery. The~translated keyboard-help text
lists these shortcuts; no~separate graphical jump buttons are~added.

The~viewer usually starts with an~appropriately sized preview. A~larger
or~original source can~be~used where the~action and~access policy call for~it.
Nearby previews may~be~prepared quietly so~the~next photograph appears smoothly.

While a~new photograph is~being prepared, the~viewer uses the~same progress area
that reports full-quality loading. Rapid forward or~backward movement always
gives the~newest requested photograph ownership of~that indicator, so~an~older
preview or~metadata request cannot leave the~viewer permanently busy or~replace
the~current selection. If~a~photograph cannot be~presented, the~progress area
shows a~recoverable failure message; the~visitor can~continue with the~forward,
backward, close, or~reopen controls. These loading states do~not~change gallery
access, protected-media, Smart Gallery, map, slideshow, or~no-JavaScript rules.

\subsection{Adjusting slideshow speed}

Start the~slideshow with \codeword{S} or~its existing control. Move the~pointer
to~reveal two compact outlined rows: \ui{Photo change speed} and~\ui{Transition
speed}. Each range offers \ui{Fast}, \ui{Medium}, and~\ui{Slow}; the~current
choice appears beside it. Both ranges run from Slow on~the~left to~Fast
on~the~right. Arrow keys adjust a~focused range without navigating photographs.
Actual input restarts the~inactivity timer. All controls, including optional
developer diagnostics, disappear after inactivity even when a~range retains
focus or~the~pointer rests over it. Dragging keeps controls visible until
release; movement reveals them again.

On~mobile, both speed ranges and~the~soundtrack panel hide with the~idle
controls. Tap the~photograph to~reveal them again; photos and~music keep
playing.

Photo display intervals are~1, 2, and~4 seconds; transition durations are~0.5,
1, and~2 seconds. Medium preserves the~existing defaults. Changes apply
immediately and~independently: the~interval replaces the~pending wait, while
the~next blend uses the~new transition duration. An~animation already running
finishes normally, and~the~next photograph still waits for~its full-quality
image to~be~ready. Choices remain during stop/resume and~close/reopen within
the~same viewer instance; reloading restores Medium. No~Save action is~needed.

\subsection{Zooming and~panning a~photograph}
\index{lightbox!zoom}
\index{zoom controls}
\index{pinch gesture}

The~lightbox zoom range is~100--400\%. Use the~minus and~plus controls,
or~select the~percentage control to~return to~100\%. Keyboard shortcuts
are~\codeword{+} or~\codeword{=} for~zoom in, \codeword{-} or~\codeword{\_}
for~zoom out, and~\codeword{0} for~reset. Focus indicators and~the~current
percentage remain exposed to~assistive technology.

On~desktop, normal mouse-wheel movement and~two-finger trackpad scrolling pan
an~enlarged photograph in~both axes; at~100\% they do~not~change the~fitted
image. A~trackpad pinch or~\codeword{Ctrl} plus wheel zooms the~photograph
around the~pointer, while \codeword{Command} plus wheel remains a~browser
shortcut. On~touchscreens, a~two-finger pinch keeps its midpoint fixed and~one
finger pans an~enlarged image. At~100\% a~one-finger horizontal swipe navigates
photographs; switching from swipe to~pinch and~from pinch to~pan
does~not~advance the~gallery.

At~100\%, the~photograph is~fitted and~centered in~the~viewer. Full-screen mode
uses the~same zoom and~pan behavior, and~its Close and~other controls remain
clickable while the~image is~enlarged.

\ui{Fit by~content} automatically hides confidently detected black borders
stored inside a~screenshot, fitting the~remaining content proportionally.
The~frame icon beside the~zoom percentage offers \ui{Show original frame};
select it~again to~restore content fit. This session choice follows navigation
and~resets zoom to~100\% when changed. Space required by~the~content's own
aspect ratio remains visible around it.
Detection reads only a~small sample of~the~active decoded image, at~most
256~pixels per~edge, without machine learning or~extra image requests.
Matched opposite borders can~support darker content edges. One edge with
sustained variation can~also confirm a~sharp brightness step beside a~flat
or~sparsely lit opposite edge. An~additional symmetry check measures both black
bands independently and~verifies sharp steps at~both boundaries with sustained
detail on~at~least one side. This also recognizes near-black scenes.
Small compression differences at~the~border can~use a~bounded shared cut;
every additional line must still pass the~black-band checks, and~both content
transitions are~verified again.
A~uniform dark fade or~a~gradual boundary
remains unchanged. Uncertain edges
and~text in~borders stay visible while other proven borders can~fit;
unavailable pixel reads keep the~whole frame. Files, thumbnails and~downloads
remain unchanged.

Manual navigation and~slideshows prepare the~crop before the~next photograph
appears. The~previous photograph retains its~geometry while loading; during
the~transition it~fades out~as~the~next one fades in. Differing image shapes
therefore do~not~leave old content visible until an~abrupt removal.
The~slideshow blend excludes the~viewer background so~removing the~incoming
cover keeps
the~same colors and~brightness, including changes between cropped and~original
frames.

During slideshow navigation, the~covered gallery retains its~modal isolation
without being reactivated between photographs. Closing the~viewer restores
the~original background state, including nested editors and~maps. Native
fullscreen retains an~opaque backing, and~touch swipe styling preserves
the~photograph fade.
In~fullscreen, the~covered page header and~cards stop painting while their
layout and~scroll position remain intact. Leaving fullscreen restores them;
nested maps and~native dialog hosts retain their own visibility. A~Smart
Gallery reuses an~unchanged source-gallery link and~updates its~address,
breadcrumb and~accessible name when the~source changes.

Zooming above 100\% immediately requests the~protected full-quality source
for~the~active photograph. The~existing preview remains visible until the~larger
image is~ready, and~the~current zoom and~pan position are~preserved. In~normal,
full-screen, and~mobile full-screen modes, this transfer shows the~same progress
bar with a~percentage and~a~byte count. Changing photographs, closing
the~viewer, or~starting a~newer full-quality request cancels the~previous
transfer. A~failed or~cancelled full-quality request leaves the~protected
preview in~place.

Navigation, slideshow start, close, and~reopen return the~viewer to~a~centered
100\%. Entering or~leaving full screen keeps the~current scale when
the~photograph itself has~not~changed and~adjusts pan to~the~new viewport.
Reduced-motion preferences remove the~short discrete zoom transition.

Zoom affects presentation only. It~does not~alter the~stored photograph
or~bypass media authorization. Password, share-link, NSFW, Smart Gallery, map,
voting, pagination, and~lightbox metadata rules continue to~apply.

\subsection{EXIF and~map policy}

Gallery maps and~the~lightbox map share compact translucent Theme chrome,
a~small title and~transparent Close action. Reset, zoom and~the~native
\ui{Keep current centered} switch wrap within one map toolbar. Fullscreen
and~slideshow split maps retain their pane geometry and~use the~same controls.
Narrow/touch actions have larger targets; route rendering, viewport memory,
provider attribution, Escape and~focus return remain available.

EXIF is~information many cameras and~phones save inside a~photograph.
It~can~include capture date, camera, lens, exposure, dimensions, orientation,
and~GPS coordinates. PHP Gallery can~use this information for~date suggestions,
maps, technical details, ordering assistance, and~duplicate review.

Maps load when a~visitor asks to~see them, which saves work for~visitors
interested only in~the~photographs. A~gallery can~show individual photo
locations and~an~overview of~available points. Parent and~child settings
determine which galleries expose this information. The~global EXIF/GPS
public-display setting is~the~fallback for~galleries whose override is~set
to~inherit. Admin also shows how many galleries currently have~an~explicit
override and~can~reset all of~those overrides back to~inherit in~one confirmed
settings save. The~reset is~schema-guarded: if~the~override state
cannot~be~verified, the~application refuses the~change and~directs
the~administrator to~System Health instead of~guessing.

Selecting \ui{Open photo} in~a~gallery-map marker opens that photograph
in~the~current lightbox when it belongs to~the~active gallery. This also works
when the~photograph is~outside the~currently displayed pagination page:
the~viewer requests only a~bounded metadata window around the~selected
photograph. In~full-screen split-map mode the~map stays open while
the~photograph pane changes. From a~normal map overlay, the~map closes
and~reveals the~selected photograph in~the~viewer. If~the~enhanced viewer cannot
resolve the~selection, the~same control follows the~canonical photograph page;
it does not~link directly to~the~raw image file.

\securitynote{GPS data can~reveal sensitive locations. Administrators should
review inherited map settings, image metadata, and~public access before
publishing personal photographs.}

A~gallery with a~saved flight route can~open its map before the~first photograph
is~uploaded, including pages with no~lightbox markup. The~map fits all stored
route points and~retains ordinary reset controls without treating route points
as~photo markers. Without stored coordinates it~shows the~normal empty-map
message. Gallery access and~effective map settings still apply; loading remains
on~demand.

Leaving photo fullscreen with \codeword{F}, its~control, or~the~browser's
\codeword{Escape} closes the~split map and~returns to~the~ordinary lightbox
with the~same photograph and~zoom. Pending map loading cannot reopen the~pane
after exit. In~CSS fullscreen, one \codeword{Escape} performs the~same
transition. \codeword{M} toggles the~map independently while fullscreen;
a~later \codeword{Escape} closes the~ordinary lightbox.

\subsection{Flight maps and~navigation data}
\index{SimBrief}
\index{flight maps}
\index{navigation data}

Admin > Maintenance > Maps and~navdata uses the~compact translucent group
treatment from~Features and~Content and~display. The~navdata name and~trailing
arrow open the~same manager. Counts and~the~last import time remain visible;
question-mark help and~Import details hold longer explanations. Update navdata
remains an~explicit action with in-place progress and~the~normal form fallback.

Flight-oriented collections can~use the~optional SimBrief integration
to~retrieve the~latest operational flight plan for~a~Pilot ID or~pilot name.
The~compact editor accepts one visible identifier: digits alone select the~Pilot
ID path; other text selects the~pilot-name path. Existing integrations that
submit separate fields remain compatible, with Pilot ID preferred when both
are~provided. The~editor creates an~editable Markdown description and~route
draft; the~OFP and~available route coordinates attach after the~gallery
is~saved. A~failed SimBrief request leaves the~ordinary description field
and~manual route editor available. The~pre-create draft is~private and~temporary
as~described in~the~gallery creation section.

The~SimBrief preview fills the~existing route editor with the~complete filed
route, adding missing departure and~arrival airports once while preserving route
instructions and~airport/runway tokens. Description drafts in~all maintained
languages keep the~complete route, including its destination on~long routes.
The~status explains that the~OFP and~available map points will attach when
you~save the~gallery; importing alone does~not~save them. If~you~change
the~route, identifier, description, or~source language while the~request runs,
the~response preserves your form values and~asks you~to~import again. After
saving, the~map retains the~original OFP coordinates.

A~gallery with a~saved local SimBrief OFP PDF offers \ui{View flight plan (OFP)}
and~\ui{Download OFP PDF} in~its description, even with no photographs. The~PDF
opens in~its own locally rendered document lightbox; its page controls never
navigate through gallery photographs. \ui{Fit whole page} is~the~initial mode,
sizing each page to~both the~usable stage width and~height and~centering it
clear of~the~header and~toolbar. Choose \ui{Fit width} for~width-first display
with vertical scrolling, or~\ui{Actual size (100\%)} for~the~PDF screen-pixel
scale. The~active mode is~indicated on~the~toolbar. Zoom in/out, 100\% zoom
reset to~the~last chosen fit, touch pinch/pan, keyboard page navigation,
and~fullscreen remain available.

\paragraph{Historical OFP attachments and pre-dispatch review.}
\index{SimBrief!retrospective OFP}
For~an existing gallery without a~valid OFP PDF, open the~administrator's
\ui{API} editor tab and~use \ui{Create historical OFP / Open prefilled
SimBrief}. A~modal first shows the~selected gallery and~editable
departure/destination ICAOs, date, UTC time, aircraft type, flight details,
route, flight level, alternate and~passengers. Verified saved OFP values
are~labelled as~such; route-map fields and~a~single gallery date are~marked
uncertain, while unknown values stay blank. The~overlay does~not~infer
an~original flight from gallery titles or~EXIF timestamps. \ui{Cancel / Back}
causes no~navigation, provider request or~gallery change.

The~review dialog also works above the~open right-hand Admin panel without
closing that editor. Its~fields remain editable, \codeword{Tab} moves between
them, and~\codeword{Escape} or~\ui{Cancel / Back} closes only the~review dialog
and~returns focus to~the~editor.

Even with~a~valid PDF already attached, \ui{Prepare revised OFP in SimBrief}
opens this~same editable review without changing the~stored document. After
generating and~downloading the~new PDF on~SimBrief, use the~separate confirmed
\ui{Replace existing OFP PDF} upload. The~\ui{Origin of the uploaded PDF}
selector only records provenance; choosing a~retrospective origin never creates
or~downloads a~file. The~departure-date picker has its~own full-width row
so~the~native control remains visible in~the~modal on~narrow screens.

Enter the~cruise flight level as~hundreds of~feet, for~example \codeword{350};
the~redirect sends the~documented \codeword{fl=FL350} representation.
An~unsigned browser reaches the~SimBrief login screen before the~dispatch
fields. Reaching that screen does~not~verify acceptance of~a~historical date;
check the~date after signing in~and~before generating.

Only \ui{Open SimBrief Dispatch} sends the~reviewed, URL-encoded options
to~SimBrief's official HTTPS dispatch page in~a~separate protected browser tab.
The~administrator may need to~sign in and~must choose \ui{Generate} on~that
site. No~developer key, OAuth or~background request is~needed. Historical dates
use the~documented DDMMMYY format, but SimBrief may reject or~adjust old dates.
A~newly generated retrospective plan is~not the~original flight-day OFP:
weather, NOTAMs, AIRAC, routes and~fuel can~differ.

After downloading the~new PDF, return to~the~same gallery's \ui{API} tab and~use
\ui{Upload OFP PDF} to~attach one validated document, at~most 25\,MiB. Select
whether it~is~a~retrospective generation or~a~manually supplied document.
A~valid existing PDF instead offers \ui{View OFP}, \ui{Download PDF},
and~a~separate collapsed \ui{Replace existing OFP PDF} workflow requiring
explicit confirmation. The~canonical OFP manifest records provenance; gallery
dates, descriptions, media, routes, visibility, and~original OFP JSON stay
unchanged. This is~a~single-gallery action, not~a~bulk rebuild.

The~saved PDF is~served through the~gallery-scoped \codeword{gallery\_ofp\_pdf}
route on~the~same browser origin as~the~gallery. Its~\codeword{index.php} query
links also work in~subdirectory installations without URL rewriting. A~fresh
anonymous visitor can~view and~download an~OFP of~a~public or~unpublished
gallery via its~direct URL without administrator login. Unpublished means
unlisted, not private: the~original PDF has the~same visibility as~the~gallery
page, including password, share-token, and~NSFW access gates. Truly private
source galleries without a~valid visitor grant and~generated private OFP-page
child galleries remain protected. A~verified administrator may access a~private
source PDF except in~anonymous preview mode. Forbidden, missing, and~invalid
PDFs return the~same uncached plain-text \codeword{404}, without rendering
an~HTML gallery page or~revealing filesystem paths. Older \codeword{media} URLs
with \codeword{ofp=1} remain compatible.

The~compact \ui{View flight plan} and~\ui{Download OFP PDF} buttons share one
row. An~administrator can~optionally create a~private OFP-page subgallery from
a~separate action below them. A~localized question-mark help button explains
the~conversion on~hover, keyboard focus, or~tap without occupying space
in~the~gallery description.

Automatic whole-page and~width fits recompute for~each PDF page's actual
dimensions and~orientation, window resizing, device rotation, and~fullscreen
transitions. Manual zoom retains its chosen CSS scale and~pan during ordinary
resizes; selecting a~fit mode or~resetting zoom restores automatic fitting.
The~automatic calculation excludes the~previous manual pan margins, so~reset
and~width/page fitting keep the~PDF readable and~proportional even after
resizing a~zoomed document. When document rendering fails, the~viewer offers
the~original protected PDF. The~existing gallery visibility, password, and~NSFW
authorization rules still guard viewing and~downloading. In~CI, the~required
Chromium job retains the~detailed \codeword{browser-map.log} as~an artifact
whenever the~document-viewer browser contract needs diagnosis.

Left and~Right arrows move by~one PDF page; holding either arrow continues
at~a~controlled rate after the~previous page finishes rendering. Navigation
stops at~the~first or~last page without wrapping. Releasing the~key, leaving
the~viewer or~changing fullscreen ends the~held-key sequence. Page navigation
preserves the~chosen fit or~manual zoom. Editable controls retain their arrow
keys; \codeword{Tab} and~\codeword{Shift+Tab} move through the~viewer controls
with visible focus, without outlining the~whole PDF stage.

The~\codeword{F} shortcut and~the~fullscreen button control only the~OFP
document viewer; \codeword{Escape} first leaves fullscreen and~then, in~normal
view, closes the~document. Typing in~editable fields, modifier shortcuts,
and~repeated keys do~not~activate fullscreen. If~the~browser refuses a~native
fullscreen request, the~same viewer uses a~CSS-only fullscreen layout.
In~fullscreen, the~header and~toolbar float above the~PDF, fade after a~short
period of~pointer inactivity, and~reappear on~pointer or~keyboard activity.
Hovered or~keyboard-focused controls remain visible; showing the~controls
does~not~resize or~rescale the~PDF.

Normal mouse-wheel movement and~two-finger trackpad scrolling pan only the~PDF
page, including horizontal scrolling and~\ui{Fit width}; they do~not~zoom
the~document, viewer controls, or~browser. \codeword{Ctrl} plus wheel
or~a~trackpad pinch zooms the~PDF at~the~pointer, while the~browser retains
\codeword{Command} plus wheel. On~phones and~tablets, one finger pans
the~document and~two fingers pinch to~zoom; the~remaining finger can~continue
panning after a~pinch. The~PDF HUD keeps a~fixed dark, high-contrast palette
independent of~the~gallery theme and~stays at~its original scale in~both normal
and~fullscreen modes. \ui{Fit whole page}, \ui{Fit width}, and~\ui{Actual size
(100\%)} restore automatic display.

Manual route text is~resolved offline-first. Imported OurAirports database rows
are~checked first, then the~small bundled CSV fallback. A~valid cached provider
result may~be~used next; when an~administrator has~linked a~configured Navigraph
account and~remote lookup is~allowed, an~unknown identifier can~be~resolved
through Navigraph and~cached under the~known AIRAC cycle. The~Navigation Data
manager shows local database and~bundled counts, provides a~lookup tester
for~identifiers such as~airports/navaids/fixes, and~can~refresh the~local
OurAirports airport/navaid dataset. Navigraph OAuth/account storage is~optional
and~can~be~disconnected without removing local data.

With JavaScript enabled, entering or~focusing a~nonempty route, requesting
a~SimBrief draft, or~saving route-bearing input also checks local
navigation-data freshness in~the~background. Both \ui{Flight Maps}
and~\ui{Navigation Data} must be~enabled. OurAirports data is~refreshed when
the~last successful import is~at~least a~week old, with an~hour between failed
attempts. Gallery saving does~not~wait for~this request; the~editor draft, open
side panel, and~browser address stay in~place. A~refresh failure still allows
the~gallery to~be~saved.

After the~check, unresolved saved manual routes can~gain missing coordinates
from~current navigation data. Existing coordinates and~SimBrief OFP geometry
are~preserved, and~a~newer route edit or~deletion prevents the~background result
from~overwriting that state. Only saved routes are~completed; unsaved browser
text is~not~sent with the~refresh. Changed public map contexts are~refreshed
through the~shared panel workflow. Import and~route writes require verified
database storage; missing or~uncertain storage prevents the~optional write while
ordinary gallery editing remains available.

Public map rendering never calls SimBrief, Navigraph, or~another live planning
service. It~renders only coordinates already stored with the~gallery. Saved
SimBrief OFP coordinates therefore remain usable even if~the~remote provider
is~later unavailable. Manual route text can~also bypass identifier lookup
completely with explicit \codeword{NAME@latitude,longitude} points.

\subsection{Voting}

Public cards and~lightbox controls share image-vote state. The~current public
vote is~a~reversible positive vote: pressing the~active up-vote again removes
it. The~displayed score is~the~sum of~stored image votes; there is~no~separate
whole-gallery voting endpoint in~the~current implementation. Logged-in votes
are~associated with the~account, while anonymous votes use the~application's
privacy-preserving visitor hash. New positive votes are~rate-limited; revoking
an~existing vote is~allowed immediately. Server-rendered forms provide direct
behavior, while JavaScript submits normal interactions asynchronously
and~synchronizes the~score across matching views. The~server validates image
identity, gallery policy, access, schema readiness, CSRF, and~request integrity.

\subsection{Picture Game}
\index{Picture Game}
\index{image comparison}

Picture Game is~an~optional public comparison mode. An~administrator enables
it~for~a~gallery branch; eligible public photographs from~that gallery and~its
descendants can~then participate subject to~the~ordinary access and~visibility
rules. The~public game shows two photographs side by~side, records which pair
was~presented, and~lets the~visitor choose the~preferred image before moving
to~the~next comparison. The~gallery can~also present leading results accumulated
by~the~game.

Because presenting a~new pair creates persistent game state even before
the~visitor chooses a~winner, Picture Game requires its database schema
to~be~known and~available. If~the~required storage cannot~be~verified, the~write
is~refused rather than~silently falling back to~an~untracked comparison.
The~global feature switch can~hide Picture Game without deleting existing game
data or~per-gallery enable settings.

\section{Duplicate Photo Detector}
\label{sec:duplicates}
\index{Duplicate Photo Detector}
\index{duplicate ledger}
\index{SHA-256}

The~Admin Duplicate Photo Detector analyzes a~selected gallery branch by~default
and~can~expand to~global scope when the~administrator enables the~corresponding
option. It~reports exact and~possible duplicate pairs, provides gallery
and~photo context links, records review decisions, and~delegates confirmed
deletion to~the~normal gallery image-deletion service.\footnote{The~detector
remains a~review tool. Evidence supports administrator judgment because matching
file size and~photographic metadata can~describe distinct photographs under some
workflows.}

\subsection{Evidence categories}

\subsubsection{Exact and~possible findings}

An~exact duplicate contains the~same file content. The~application recognizes
this through a~checksum, which acts like a~very detailed digital fingerprint
calculated from the~file. Matching fingerprints provide strong evidence that two
files contain the~same bytes.

A~possible duplicate has~supporting similarities without an~exact fingerprint
match. The~detector can~compare file size and~available camera information such
as~dimensions, capture time, camera, lens, exposure, aperture, focal length,
ISO, and~orientation. Two separately exported copies of~one photograph
may~differ as~files while retaining enough shared information to~deserve review.

Missing camera information simply gives the~detector fewer clues. The~results
remain suggestions for~a~person to~inspect. Open both photograph links, compare
their gallery context and~quality, and~delete only the~copy whose removal fits
the~collection.

\subsection{Persistent review ledger}

The~ledger belongs to~the~authenticated administrator. Pair entries store
canonical image identifiers, with the~lower identifier in~the~low column
and~the~higher identifier in~the~high column. Gallery entries apply to~the~exact
stored gallery. Parent and~child gallery decisions remain independent.

Available review actions include:

\begin{itemize}
  \item ignore the~selected pair;
  \item ignore all findings from the~left image's exact gallery;
  \item ignore all findings from the~right image's exact gallery;
  \item clear the~current administrator's ledger;
  \item delete a~confirmed duplicate through validated normal image deletion.
\end{itemize}

AJAX actions refresh the~detector fragment in~the~existing Admin right-side
panel. Direct POST requests use redirect behavior as~a~fallback. Server-owned
scan jobs preserve scope between continuation requests, and~deletion prunes
the~affected image from current job results.

\section{Access control and~security}
\label{sec:security}
\index{security}
\index{CSRF}
\index{authentication}
\index{NSFW}

\subsection{Layered access evaluation}

Privacy is~evaluated before protected output is~assembled. Gallery visibility
and~inherited access rules are~combined with per-photo visibility,
password/share-link state, and~age-restriction rules where configured. The~same
authorization applies to~gallery pages, thumbnails, previews, original files,
lightbox metadata, maps, search results, and~downloads.

Admin operations require an~authenticated account. Forms include a~private
request token, and~the~server validates the~target gallery, photograph,
and~requested operation before changing persistent data.\footnote{The~technical
name for~the~private form token is~a~CSRF token. Gallery owners normally
encounter it~only through ordinary Admin forms.}

\subsection{Passwords and~share links}
\index{password protection}
\index{share links}

Protected galleries store password hashes and~grant session-scoped access after
successful verification. Share links use server-generated token state and~expiry
policy. Password reset uses one-time hashed tokens, an~optional recovery email,
configured lifetime, and~the~selected mail transport. Use HTTPS, strong
administrator credentials, restricted database accounts, and~protected mail/API
secrets.

A~listed password-protected gallery can~appear as~a~locked card with its title
and~link; the~locked card does~not~show its description or~cover image.

Administrator cookie protection, generated URL schemes and~viewer HTTPS checks
share one transport policy. Direct TLS is~recognized without proxy settings.
When a~reverse proxy terminates TLS and~forwards HTTP to~PHP, configure its
exact IP address or~CIDR in~\codeword{security.trusted_proxies} and~enable
the~actual protocol header in~\codeword{security.trusted_proxy_protocol_headers}
before upgrading. Supported headers are~\codeword{x-forwarded-proto}
and~\codeword{x-forwarded-ssl}; client-IP header trust is~configured separately.
The~proxy must overwrite client-supplied values in~enabled headers. Untrusted,
malformed, multiple or~conflicting protocol assertions cannot prove HTTPS. Both
configured proxy hosting and~direct HTTPS remain supported; forwarded headers
alone no~longer upgrade public URLs or~set the~administrator cookie Secure flag.

\subsection{Viewer accounts and~invite-only registration}
\index{viewer accounts}
\index{viewer invitations}
\index{viewer account management}
\index{viewer login}

Viewer accounts are~an~optional identity layer for~personalized gallery
features. They are~separate from~the~administrator account and, by~themselves,
do~not~unlock a~private or~password-protected gallery. Existing gallery
passwords, share links, Smart Gallery access, and~media authorization continue
to~decide what a~visitor may see.

The~complete viewer-account subsystem is~wrapped by~a~master switch
in~\ui{Admin > Features}. This~master feature is~disabled by~default. While
it~is~off, the~public gallery shows no~viewer Login/Account controls, viewer
routes are~unavailable, and~the~\ui{Viewer accounts} entry is~hidden
from~the~Admin Account menu. Existing viewer identities, favourites, and~private
collections remain stored; the~switch controls reachability rather than~deleting
data.

After enabling the~master feature, the~administrator can~open \ui{Account >
Viewer accounts}. That~page retains the~separate \ui{Viewer accounts
(invite-only)} mode switch. The~mode switch controls viewer login/invitations
inside the~enabled subsystem, while the~master Features switch controls whether
the~subsystem is~exposed at~all.

An~administrator manages viewer identities from \ui{Account > Viewer accounts}.
The~existing invite-only workflow remains available: the~administrator may
optionally bind an~invitation to~an~intended email address and~copy
its~show-once secret link. Opening the~link does~not~create an~account.
The~recipient submits an~email address, receives a~verification message through
the~configured mail transport, confirms the~verification in~the~browser,
and~then chooses a~password of~at~least 15~characters. There is~no~general
\ui{Register} or~\ui{Sign up} page.

The~same Admin page can~also create a~viewer account directly. Enter the~viewer
email and~either provide a~temporary password that meets the~viewer password
policy or~leave the~password blank so~the~gallery generates a~strong one.
The~temporary password is~shown once after creation. If~the~optional
account-created notification is~sent, the~message contains the~trusted login
address and~instructions but~never the~temporary password; provide that password
separately through a~trusted channel. With the~master feature enabled, direct
account creation remains available while the~subordinate viewer frontend mode
is~disabled so~accounts can~be~prepared in~advance, but those viewers cannot
sign in~until that~viewer mode is~enabled.

A~directly created viewer cannot use the~temporary password as~a~durable login.
After the~first successful credential check, the~browser is~sent to~the~private
first-login page and~must choose a~different compliant password. Until that
replacement succeeds, the~gallery does~not~establish the~normal viewer identity,
issue \ui{Remember me}, or~allow viewer favourites/collections authority.
The~ordinary forgotten-password reset can~also replace the~temporary credential
through its~independently verified recovery flow. A~simultaneous administrator
login in~the~same browser remains independent.

Existing viewer accounts are~listed on~the~Admin page with~their account state
and~security controls. An~active viewer can~be~suspended with~\ui{Suspend}
or~signed out everywhere; a~suspended viewer can~be~reactivated
with~\ui{Restore}. Suspension blocks viewer authentication and~invalidates
the~viewer's existing session, remember-me, reset, pending
verification/email-change, and~prepared collection-share authority through
the~central viewer lifecycle service. Restoration makes the~account active again
but~does not~revive any credential or~session that existed before suspension,
so~the~viewer must authenticate again normally. A~forced first-login password
replacement remains required across suspend/restore. \ui{Sign out everywhere}
leaves an~active account active while revoking its~viewer login authority on~all
devices. These security controls remain available to~the~administrator while
the~master feature is~enabled but~the~subordinate viewer frontend mode
is~disabled. Turning the~master feature off hides the~viewer-management surface
as~well. None of~these switches or~security controls removes favourites, private
collections, collection items, photographs, galleries, Smart Galleries, gallery
share links, or~administrator authentication.

Admin deletion remains a~separate destructive action with explicit confirmation.
It~removes the~viewer identity and~viewer-owned authentication, lifecycle,
